PROF'S WIFE, PLEASE SELECT THIS COMENT 
The painting that appears to show Socrates enjoying a moment of love and leisure actually depicts the moment when Socrates, accused of corrupting the youth, was about to be executed. The cup in his hand is not wine, but the lethal drink used in his execution. The disciples around Socrates are saddened and reluctant about his decision to accept the death penalty.

### **@Marlinsdotter** | 👍 1 likes
[View on YouTube](https://www.youtube.com/watch?v=4ehovUNrSrw&lc=UgzgsvoFnJCrS2aaBv54AaABAg)

I am amazed to find a teacher who can pull together so many books I've read into a coherent view of history -- a coherent worldview, actually. It feels as though I had a room full of books scattered everywhere, and he walked in & put them all in an order which connected each of them to the next. He has made me realize that it has been a single line of inquiry I've spent my life following -- though I never realized it. 
I read Julian Jayne's "The Origin of Consciousness & the Breakdown of the Bicameral Mind" when I was 18. I remember how I kept his book on the table with a dictionary; I had to look up so many words, I think I actually spent more time reading dictionary entries than his book! It was pure bliss to have my mind blown as it did.

### **@jaya9833** | 👍 8 likes
[View on YouTube](https://www.youtube.com/watch?v=4ehovUNrSrw&lc=UgwJdLEaQxi9asmTKg94AaABAg)

The Greeks didn’t create the alphabet, they adopted it. There are several other wrong info here as well, but this one is glaring. Most people see Greeks through the European history books that glorified Greeks as a pure “European” civilization, while no such a thing as “European” existed. They were part of the “Mediterranean” civilization, and they borrowed heavily from their neighboring city states. Many of the surrounding civilizations also moved into Greece to make up the population. Same goes for Roman Empire, which eventually invaded and tamed the “Barbarian” Northern lands, which were to the North of Italy. The European proper eventually integrated into rest of civilization when Charlemagne invaded all of Western Europe and forcefully unified it. The “Holy Roman Empire” followed afterwards.

### **@daniellerosegoesupinflames**
[View on YouTube](https://www.youtube.com/watch?v=4ehovUNrSrw&lc=UgxuZAg34D3y_JGzDSV4AaABAg)

My grandmother and I are very close. She raised me and we both have always felt that we have a special bond. It happens to us both all the time. One of us will have a sudden thought of the other and pick up the phone to call as it starts ringing because the other is calling. Sometimes if I’m busy I will think to myself I need to call her today and almost always she calls within minutes. It would be easy to explain this away if we talked on the phone routinely but we don’t. It has happened since I was little, when we didn’t have cell phones and even when we lived on opposite ends of the country. The older I get the more strongly I feel that people in the past were able to perceive and sense things that we can’t today.

I love history and i just wish i had such a good teacher like Prof. Jiang, i had to learn almost everything by myself and this is coming from a country which is seen as one of the world leading education systems - I had such a bad teacher who tormented me and my class mates for years because she refused to retire, we had same school books for years and it was very repetitive teaching, nothing new, always the same stuff for almost 5 years. - Only time i was truly excited was when we opened history book. I dont understand how Finnish school board let this clearly insane teacher to continue working...She did absolutely nothing to make us excited about school, she was just enjoying her last years of work by just sitting and making us watch VHS tapes or go play sports. She was so insane that she forced us to sit inside classroom dividers to isolate us from each other so we couldnt communicate with each other, if we did, we would get punishment. On top of that we were left inside a rotten school when everyone else moved to another school and we were left to this huge empty sinking school (the school was literally sinking every year and thats why it was demolished years later).  - Me and my old class mates have talked often about this teacher and how crazy she was and how we did not learn ANYTHING during these years. Our education system totally failed and left us to school which was full of mold, there was always air tests to check is it good enough to KIDS to stay in school which was known to have mold. 
From grade 4 to 9 i didnt learn not much at all, after 9 grade i was so glad to get rid of her insane behaviour and continued to vocational school which was fun but years of not learning anything affected my studies very badly.

Jiang denied all such; all so that the world could fit into his little mould. In other word, he picked one piece of art which he didn't like and say it was considered the greatest by the world, to eventually conclude that the world is bad. This is fallacy manifested.

So no, we are not going to collapse anytime soon. We won the time lottery and get to live in the most prosperous and humane period of human history so far. We will stumble and fall in the future, but we will continue moving forward, and things will get better.

### **@QuickTimeVelocity**
[View on YouTube](https://www.youtube.com/watch?v=4ehovUNrSrw&lc=Ugy3btxJotc1H77nO4V4AaABAg)

My answers to the questions starting at 33:00:

1. I don't recall it ever happening. I find if it ever does, it is only coincidence, thought one that may be common amongst people in general.
2. This I don't, but I find can be induced in various ways and circumstances pertinent to your state of mind and environment.
3. Bodily, no. Mentally, yes. This of course upon reflective observation of what's going on in my head will show that it comes from external stimuli and passive feelings that arise from them and the thoughts that come or leave because of such stimuli. 
4. I find this to be possible due to the subconscious. I find that many thoughts and ideas that we process can end up slipping into the subconscious when dormant long enough, but nonetheless still going on to some degree to which we're not aware of. For these ideas to "pop up" I think is the point in which it returns to full consciousness, usually in conjunction with some kind of stimuli that triggers it (similar to remembering throughout the day bits and pieces of a dream you had the night before).
5. Yes, this one does happen for me. I find these ideas to come out of boredom, more often than not.
6. Likely the same thing with the state of boredom, but for the subconscious, given our conscious self is not active when asleep. As for meaning, that one you may need professional insight for.
7. I have some theories of telepathy, but I'd rather not get into them here. But yes, I do.
8. Not myself, but for those who do, I would assume it comes out of cravings for company.
9. This one I feel is subject to one's spiritual beliefs or even imagination, so I leave this one to those who hold one or the other on the subject.

This comes from someone without formal education in psychology; I speak only from insights I've read of from a couple of books on the subject.

“You have heard that it was said, ‘Love your neighbor[a] and hate your enemy.’ But I tell you, love your enemies and pray for those who persecute you, that you may be children of your Father in heaven. He causes his sun to rise on the evil and the good, and sends rain on the righteous and the unrighteous.  If you love those who love you, what reward will you get? ### **@fotispap9**
[View on YouTube](https://www.youtube.com/watch?v=4ehovUNrSrw&lc=UgzhyhXGlnqPPvb3RMd4AaABAg)

I can understand the theory of the left and right hemispheres (logical–emotional) only schematically and abstractly, as a duality within the human psyche. In reality, the hemispheres are interconnected through the diencephalon, and there are also centers that are not symmetrical and occupy unique anatomical positions. Beyond that, however, it seems paradoxical to begin from a biological basis in order to explain philosophical concepts.
I believe it is important, first and foremost, to question the very notions of egoism and altruism themselves—concepts that are not inherent in our nature but are morally constructed by culture. What is at stake is a rupture with existing relations of power and morality, within which egoism may turn against other lives, while altruism, when expressed from a position of authority, may also become a means of control.
Another reference I would like to introduce concerns the question of ancient Greek civilization. The city-states were originally organized hierarchically and were founded upon the division between slaves and citizens. For Athenian citizens to engage in public speech, others had to labor continuously.

Also In the ancient theaters, tragedies also served a function of governance: they shaped the identity of the citizen and produced universal moral values.

### **@chalinofalcone871**
[View on YouTube](https://www.youtube.com/watch?v=4ehovUNrSrw&lc=Ugy8J_BZX03BsdyeVwx4AaABAg)

"There is a basic principle that distinguishes a hot medium like radio from a cool one like the telephone, or a hot medium like the movie from a cool one like TV. A hot medium is one that extends one single sense in "high definition." High definition is the state of being well filled with data...
Hot media are, therefore, low in participation, and cool media are high in participation or completion by the audience...
A cool medium like hieroglyphic or ideogrammic  written characters has very different effects from the hot and explosive medium of the phonetic alphabet."
[Understanding Media: The Extension of Man, Marshall McLuhan, 1964, Ch. 2: Hot Media and Cold]

> @krunkle5136 You are talking about an ideal, but I think this is simply a practical matter: If something is easier than something else, it is more likely to happen than the other. If learning 26 letters is a lot easier than memorizing 80 thousand ideograms, many of which may be untranslatable to your language, then choice is a no--brainer. But ironically so,  choosing the naive, short symbol set over the sea of calligraphy empowers a lot more people to express and communicate ideas, making everybody wiser and possibly interested in calligraphy. I think it was in another episode of the series that the professor said that ideograms, being so difficult to master, were the province of scribes, that typically were employed by the powerful, and not the common people. And so it comes that the "naive" alphabet gives birth to deep thinking and philosophy  and mathematics and geometry and physics and eventually democracy, all in the span of a mere three centuries. Sorry, but you cannot get all that and so quickly using thousands of ideograms. ### **@terykrali196**
[View on YouTube](https://www.youtube.com/watch?v=4ehovUNrSrw&lc=UgwTyhfjjnmmZxb39JN4AaABAg)

By dragging Hector’s body behind his chariot, Achilles commits one of the most serious crimes in ancient Greek eyes: **grievous hybris and sacrilege against a corpse (ὕβρις εἰς νεκρόν).
Defiling a dead body was considered worse than murder itself, because it offended the gods, the unwritten laws, human dignity, and polluted the whole community with miasma.
That’s why the gods themselves intervene and force Achilles to return the body to Priam.

### **@mariokman21**
[View on YouTube](https://www.youtube.com/watch?v=4ehovUNrSrw&lc=UgzgXnE-U-e8rR0JtJB4AaABAg)

Prof, I want to say that you don’t necessarily have to be a pessimist when it comes to the world around you. I agree with everything you’re saying except a few things. I do not think current art is shit, in fact quite the opposite. There is so much good art nowadays and so much art that can move you based on the collective consciousness of present day. The Iliad is an amazing piece of writing, and so were the rest of the Greek epics, and so were many Hindu epics such as the Mahabharata. As far as I am concerned, these stories are adaptations of true events that happened in that time period. But there are so many pieces of great art today. When it comes to music, movies, books, video games, community. And the amazing thing is we can have discussions about all these things as we are on the internet right now. I don’t necessarily think a world where the collective consciousness is aware of the gods is better than when the consciousness is not. Instead of the Monad, or the Brahman, or any other name, many spiritual people today call it the universe. We have to understand that truth is one, no matter what name any civilization gives it.

I wonder if you are aware of something called the great year or the yuga cycle (not the orthodox Hindu version but Sri Yukteswar’s version in his book the holy science). Essentially the great year is a framework of history that divides it up into 4 distinct ages across 24,000 years. The closer we are to the top of the cycle, the closer human consciousness is to the spirit or the monad. The further we are, the closer we are to satan or the material world. If you want literature about this, I suggest the holy science and the yugas by Joseph Selbie. The Iliad and the Mahabharata were produced in the descending Bronze Age or Dwapara yuga, and according to this cycle, we are currently in the neo-bronze age or an ascending Dwapara. Eventually, human beings will reach a point where we all will collectively understand the spirit, but this will be a slow and gradual process over the next 7000 years or so. This science was actually discovered in ancient times across many different cultures, but devolved into nonsense as time went on.

What I am trying to say is that through a truthful analysis of history, you can discover spirituality and consciousness, just as you have. But don’t be so quick to discount the progress we have made in modern times. Through centuries of war and turmoil, we have reached the point where people are not completely miserable to live their lives. I truly believe we are through the worst of times. Just think how many millions of lives were lost in the past centuries due to ignorance. Of course, things are not perfect and we still have a long way to go and I’m not saying civilizations will not collapse and people won’t die at all, but I’m saying there is reason to be hopeful.

### **@sergbob772**
[View on YouTube](https://www.youtube.com/watch?v=4ehovUNrSrw&lc=UgxQgdcvhqKHBluT2Mh4AaABAg)

Homer's examples of "divine intervention" are similar to the usual literary / dramatic reception: "God from a Machine." That is, when in a Greek theater a "god" is lowered onto the stage by a special mechanism (machine) and  God resolves all conflicts in the play, unravels plot moves invented by the playwright, reconciles lovers, punishes villains, rewards kind and sweet, etc. "God from the Machine" is a bad trick in creativity, from impotence. And Homer, in the excerpts given in the lecture, just used it for the same purposes: "let well enough" so i will do it. That is, instead of prescribing the motivation of the hero, the reason why Achilles refrained from killing Agamemnon, Homer "stuck" Athena. Why, it's convenient and fast! By the way, the huge Greek amphitheaters explain why the Greeks so often used the bad "God from the machine" technique, because the performances/entertainments were very frequent, mb, almost daily, for this you need to write a lot of plays, and writing a lot does not work well. That's the solution: lets add "special effects", it will do, they will like it.

The Angel Who Let the Apple Fall from the Tree 

It came to pass that an apple fell from the tree. Isaac did not turn his eyes toward the earth, but toward the abyss that yawned within his heart. The fruit taught him nothing; it only stirred the knowledge he had carried before time.

It was not intuition—it was the memory of the first breath. Isaac was born Adam, not the first, but the heir of the first. And the Book awakened within him.

Raziel had already inscribed it upon his breath. And history repeated itself, under another name. He entered the world with the Book open, not to read it, but to have drunk it before he even drew breath.

He discovered nothing—he remembered, like a child who hums a lullaby never taught.

The key dwelled in his spirit, and he saw the world in its hidden truth. Yet men, calling themselves wise, shattered that spirit. Thus the perdition foretold by Enoch came to pass: the apple became law, the law became machine, and the machine became bomb. Enoch had written it. The angels transmitted the knowledge. Men forged the sword.

Adam received the Book. They believed he had come to sow gardens. But he was there to forge the end. It was not punishment, but revelation.

MartOn Nov 08, 2025

Just a nitpick:  Menelaus was in fact only king of Sparta by virtue of his marriage to Helen, who was queen and descended from (actually adopted by) the Spartan royal family.  Menelaus was Agamemnons younger brother, and both from the royal house of Mycenae.  So Helen being kidnapped by Paris (she probably went willingly!) meant Menelaus had no real link to the Sparta throne.  So this gives both brothers good reason to get her back.

Ancient Egyptians, who were dark-skinned Africans, have been systematically portrayed as light-skinned or “Caucasian” in art, academic works, and museum displays, erasing their African identity. This racial rewriting was coupled with sidelining Egypt’s role as an intellectual cradle. Greek philosophers such as Plato and Pythagoras extensively studied Egyptian knowledge in philosophy, mathematics, and ethics, yet their African sources were minimized or ignored. The Greek philosophical tradition was framed as original, obscuring its deep debt to Egyptian-African wisdom, which allowed European narratives to claim intellectual primacy while denying African contributions.

> **@thereisnoend**  
> [View on YouTube](https://www.youtube.com/watch?v=4ehovUNrSrw&lc=Ugyp23gDa4283l1-Yz54AaABAg.APBOrAXOb-0APBOwrSJ4Dw)  
>
> Mainstream scholarship has often ignored or reinterpreted archaeological and biological evidence that affirms the African identity of ancient Egyptians and the African roots of Greek philosophy. Studies showing cultural, linguistic, and intellectual continuities between Kemet (Egypt) and early Greek thought have been marginalized or reframed to fit a Mediterranean-centric narrative that excludes dark-skinned Africans. This selective scholarship has facilitated the appropriation of African knowledge by Greek philosophy and perpetuated the erasure of Africa’s role as the cradle of many foundational ideas.

> @mdiederichsen The Phoenicians did not have an alphabet, they had what is called an adjad, i.e. consonants only, with the vowels inferred by reading the text in context (as I read is also the case with Hebrew and Arabic). Socrates: thinks in terms of the True and eternal good life. Realm of forms. Thinks before he acts but not for himself but for the good of the people and mankind and not out of his own will but because its all this is, bringing ppl closer to the eternal and all good through dialectic.

greek life started at Achilles, then that led to Odysseyus. Then that led to Socrates. That transformation can also be described as the life of a person, starting at infant, to young man, To Man.

 Then Rome said "what do you think about this?" and sent a spear through the Greeks head.
